10ShellAn oversized program needlessly swaps out a resident process300-byte load: PID 0 rolled to disk, then “insufficient memory” → “program is too large”, nothing swapped9
11Shellps reports a swapped-out process as Mem 0location=Mem 0 → location=Disk17
12SchedulerFCFS (and PRIORITY) still preempt every quantum, so they behave like RRsetschedule fcfs + three 12-instruction programs: 4 context switches, finish order 0, 2, 1 → 0 switches, order 0, 1, 213
13SchedulerEvery process has priority 0, and runall picks the next process without consulting the policyload 5, load 1, load 3 under PRIORITY now finish in the order 1, 3, 513
14DiskTwo files created back to back share one data blockcreate a; create b; write a "hello"; read b prints hello → prints nothing15
15DiskNames of 22–30 characters are accepted, truncated to 21 on disk, and can never be found againcreate abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxy “succeeds” → rejected15
16Diskchkdsk can never recover a deleted file, because delete zeroes the pointercreate; write; delete; chkdsk: 0 recovered → 1 recovered, as RECOVERED_TSB15
17SwapperEvery roll-in leaks its swap image in sessionStoragefour-process run: 4 images left behind → 017

Not fixed: design gaps

These are larger than a patch, and each is discussed (with a sketch of the fix) in its chapter.

GapWhy it mattersCh.
Files are one block: at most 30 characters, and write silently truncates while recording the full length as the sizeLab 100’s long-string write; the whole allocation-methods discussion; swap images couldn’t fit on the disk because of it15
Swap images live in separate sessionStorage keys, not on the TSB diskLab 100’s “using the disk as a backing store for your swapper”; invisible in the disk viewer17
format -quick zeroes 21 hex digits of every block, not “just the first four bytes,” destroying data and directory metadataquick-formatted data can’t be recovered15
link copies size and date into the new directory entry, so a write through one name leaves the other’s size stalemetadata belongs to the file, not the name (the inode idea)14
The directory is a single sector: 8 files maximumsingle-level directory; the other 56 blocks of track 0 are unused14, 15
The disk panel re-parses the whole JSON disk image 256 times per clock tickcost; a block cache would fix it3, 15a
The mode bit is informational and nests without a stacknothing enforces it; “user mode” while kernel code runs6
krnTrap (BSOD) and krnTrapError (no BSOD) duplicate each otheran unknown IRQ shuts down without a BSOD6
Shell touches the DOM directly (status, load)crosses the host/OS layer boundary8
Interrupts are FIFO, not prioritized by IRQthe timer can wait behind a burst of keystrokes6, 13a
Unused methods (releaseByBase, isAllocated, availableCapacity, releaseAllocation, getPartitionForPid, allocateSwapBlock, releaseBlock, readBytes)surface area to read and maintain11, 17

Building with TypeScript 6 or later

rObSen was written against TypeScript 5, under which it compiles cleanly. TypeScript 6 (and the native TypeScript 7 compiler) change two defaults that matter here: strict is on unless you turn it off, and declaring a namespace with the old module keyword is an error. Under those versions the unmodified project reports 147 errors (it still emits JavaScript, so it still runs). Two small changes restore a clean build:

  1. Replace module TSOS { with namespace TSOS { in the 18 files that use it (Chapter 1a explains why they mean the same thing).
  2. Add "strict": false to compilerOptions in tsconfig.json, or, better, keep strict mode on and work through the errors it reports; most are the implicit-any parameters and unchecked nulls described in Chapters 3a and 6a.

D. Testing with GLaDOS

GLaDOS (“It’s for testing. And enrichment.”) is Alan’s automated tester, named after the homicidal AI of Portal. Each test/glados-ip*.js file defines a global function Glados with two methods, init and afterStartup. If index.html includes one of the files, Control.hostInit notices the function (typeof Glados === "function") and creates her, and at the end of krnBootstrap the kernel calls _GLaDOS.afterStartup(). From then on she types at your OS.

How she types

She doesn’t fake browser events. She writes characters straight onto the kernel input queue and then calls the interrupt handler with an Enter keystroke:

_KernelInputQueue.enqueue("v");
_KernelInputQueue.enqueue("e");
_KernelInputQueue.enqueue("r");
TSOS.Kernel.prototype.krnInterruptHandler(KEYBOARD_IRQ, [13, false]);

The interrupt handler runs the keyboard driver (which decodes key 13 and enqueues a carriage return), then _StdIn.handleInput() drains the queue: v, e, r are echoed, and the CR hands ver to the shell. This bypasses the host keyboard, the interrupt queue, and the clock. Chapter 10a explains why calling the handler on Kernel.prototype instead of _Kernel works (the kernel keeps all its state in globals).

For program tests, she puts hex into the User Program Input textarea (document.getElementById("taProgramInput").value = codeA) and types load, with setTimeout spacing the steps a second or more apart so the OS has time to boot and run.

The test files

FileTestsHighlights
glados-ip0.jsProject Zerohelp, ver
glados-ip1.jsPart Onehelp, ver, date, whereami, status The Cake is a lie! (watch the case; see Chapter 8), several loads with valid and invalid input
glados-ip2.jsPart Twoload, run 0
glados-ip3.jsPart Threefour timed loads, then runall: exercises partitions, the scheduler, and what happens when a fourth program arrives with memory full
glados-ip4.jsPart Fourformat, getschedule, five loads (so swapping is required), runall, then file-system commands
glados-ip4x.jsPart Four, “EXTREME swapping”ver, format, then twenty programs loaded one second apart, then runall

index.html ships with glados-ip4x.js enabled, so every page load starts the extreme swapping test. Comment out that <script> line to boot to a quiet prompt.

What ip4x’s programs do

All twenty programs are the same 6502a code with a different letter in the data area. Disassembled (the start of one):

00: A9 00      LDA #$00
02: 8D 7B 00   STA $007B
…
1B: EC 7A 00   CPX $007A
1E: D0 39      BNE $59
20: A0 7D      LDY #$7D
22: A2 02      LDX #$02
24: FF         SYS            ; print the string at $7D (the program's letter)
25: AC 7B 00   LDY $007B
28: A2 01      LDX #$01
2A: FF         SYS            ; print a counter
…

Each prints its letter and a counter in a loop, and finally "<letter>done" from $7F. With twenty of them and three partitions, seventeen live on disk at any moment, so nearly every context switch is a roll-out plus a roll-in. If the swapper or the dispatcher has a bug, ip4x finds it. The November 9 commit that switched PCBs to logical program counters (Chapter 4) was a bug “uncovered by GLaDOS.”

Writing your own GLaDOS

Because the harness is just JavaScript calling into your OS, you can write targeted regression tests the same way. Every bug in Appendix C was found and confirmed with a small Node.js harness in this style: load the compiled distrib/*.js files into one context with stub document, sessionStorage, and canvas objects, replace _StdOut.putText with a function that records output, then call _OsShell.handleInput("…") and TSOS.Devices.hostClockPulse() directly. For example:

cmd("format"); cmd("create a"); cmd("create b");
cmd('write a "hello"'); cmd("read b");
// expected: nothing; rObSen prints "hello"  →  two files share one data block

That’s GLaDOS’s idea, without a browser: drive the OS through its own entry points, and check what comes out.
